```markdown
| Section | Content                                                                                                           |
|---------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 8.6.2   | Other devices dysfunction. However, newer classes have neutral effects.                                          |
|         | Most device-based therapies investigated for BP-lowering therapy in hypertension have been aimed at modulating the autonomic nervous system activity (baroreflex activation therapy, endovascular flex amplification therapy, and carotid body ablation). |
|         | Cardiac neuromodulation therapy aims to lower left ventricular preload by variably altering the atrioventricular interval using a dual-chamber, rate-responsive, implantable pulse generator in patients indicated for implantation or replacement of a dual-chamber pacemaker. |
|         | Some, though not all, of these devices have shown promising results in non-randomized, single-arm studies. A sham-controlled trial gating central iliac arteriovenous stent anastomosis was terminated early after longer-term follow-up data indicated an increase in heart failure in the stent group. Therefore, the use of these device-based therapies is not recommended for routinely treating hypertension until further evidence regarding their safety and efficacy becomes available. |
| 8.7.2   | Pill burden and non-adherence. More intensive treatment of elevated BP and hypertension may be associated with an increased risk of polypharmacy and pill burden, which are themselves associated with non-adherence. Single-pill, dose drug combinations can help to reduce pill burden and are recommended to improve adherence. Increased intensity of BP lowering can also result in higher upfront direct and indirect healthcare costs, with more people requiring medication and higher demand for technology-based adherence strategies, which can be challenging to implement, especially in resource-poor settings. |
| 8.7.3   | Potentially harmful consequences of blood pressure lowering for frail older people. Unintended consequences of BP lowering (hypotension, syncope, falls) can be hazardous for frail older people in particular. Retrospective studies have shown that adults aged >75 years from the general population, who would have met the criteria for inclusion in the Systolic Blood Pressure Intervention Trial (SPRINT), had a rate of injurious falls and cope that was nearly five times that of the standard care group in the trial. This suggests that healthy participant bias may have contributed to the findings of SPRINT and other similar BP-lowering trials, and that the results may not fully generalize to older adults in more routine clinical care. Patients’ functional ability should be considered in addition to age to help negate any unintended consequences of BP lowering in a frailer cohort. Despite their chronological age, older patients with hypertension who are fit and can independently carry out activities of daily living will benefit from guideline-directed treatment similar to younger cohorts. However, tailoring treatment targets and treatment plans for frail older patients is necessary to avoid unintended consequences. This should include assessing frailty, including cognitive status, risk of falls, propensity for symptomatic orthostatic hypotension, polypharmacy, and other morbid conditions. Of note, some data indicate a benefit of more intensive BP-lowering on cognitive function. For those with loss of function but preserved activities of daily living, a more detailed geriatric assessment is required to explore the risks and benefits of treatment, as well as considerations for tailoring therapeutic strategies where appropriate. For patients who are both functionally impaired and unable to carry out activities of daily living, the therapeutic goals of hypertension treatment should be personalized, and medications discontinued where appropriate. |
| 8.7.4   | Clinical inertia in blood pressure lowering. The fear of serious adverse events with BP-lowering medications is often cited as a reason for clinical inertia, although the evidence to date from meta-analyses of RCTs suggests these side-effect concerns may be exaggerated. However, RCTs often select populations with less frailty and multimorbidity who are more likely to tolerate treatment. Consequently, fewer adverse effects might be reported than would be expected in the general population. It remains up to individual clinicians to initiate shared decision-making with each patient, especially patients in vulnerable groups and those who have experienced previous adverse events, weighing up potential benefits against risks of treatment. |
| 8.7.1.1 | Symptomatic adverse effects. BP-lowering medications have multiple side effects, which may be more common in females. Although generally well tolerated, common side effects include headaches, cough, dizziness or light-headedness, diarrhea or constipation, fatigue, ankle swelling, and erectile problems, pending on the drug class. In randomized trials of adults aged >60 years, the overall rate of symptomatic BP-lowering drug withdrawal was higher than the rate of placebo withdrawal (approximately 15% vs. 5%). A systematic view, which included 280,638 participants in 58 RCTs, reported no evidence for an increased relative risk of falls in those taking BP-lowering drugs. There was, however, an increased relative risk of mild hyperkalemia, acute kidney injury, hypotension, and syncope. Furthermore, very frail adults were excluded from BP-lowering trials, which is important because such patients are more prone to adverse effects and polypharmacy. |
| 8.7.1.2 | Renal effects. A systematic review reported an increased risk of acute kidney injury and hyperkalemia associated with BP-lowering treatment. Analyses of outcomes by specific drug class showed that drugs affecting the RAAS were more likely to be associated with acute kidney injury and hyperkalemia. Patients with significant CKD tend to be excluded from RCTs. It is important to remember these exclusion criteria, and that patients with CKD are more likely to suffer from resistant hypertension when extrapolating the results of more intensive BP lowering to patients with moderate-to-severe CKD. |
| 8.7.1.3 | Erectile dysfunction. Older classes of BP-lowering drugs (including diuretics, beta-blockers, and centrally acting medications) are associated with erectile dysfunction. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Assess patient for hypertension.
- If hypertension is present, proceed to step 2.
- If not, monitor and reassess periodically.

2. Determine treatment goals.
- Consider patient age, comorbidities, and functional ability.
- Tailor treatment targets for frail older patients.

3. Initiate BP-lowering therapy.
- Choose appropriate medication based on patient profile.
- Monitor for side effects and adherence.

4. Evaluate treatment response.
- If BP is controlled, continue current regimen.
- If BP is not controlled, consider adjusting therapy.

5. Assess for adverse effects.
- Monitor for hypotension, syncope, falls, and renal effects.
- If adverse effects occur, reassess treatment plan.

6. Consider polypharmacy and pill burden.
- Simplify regimen with single-pill combinations if necessary.
- Educate patient on adherence strategies.

7. Reassess treatment periodically.
- Adjust therapy based on patient’s changing health status.
- Engage in shared decision-making with the patient.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| Section | Content |
|---------|---------|
| 9       | Managing specific patient groups |
| 9.1     | Young adulthood (18–40 years) |
| 9.1.1   | Definition and epidemiology |
|         | In the present guidelines ‘young adulthood’ is defined as age 18–40 years. Irrespective of cardiovascular risk, all young adults with elevated BP are recommended to follow lifestyle guidance for BP lowering. A discussion about family planning should be taken with young women of childbearing potential at each visit. Adherence to treatment is low in young adults, &#x3C;50% in some studies. Therefore, communicating the importance of adherence, education, and follow-up clinics is important. |
| 9.1.2   | Secondary hypertension in young adulthood |
|         | Secondary hypertension is more frequent in younger than in older onset hypertension, with a prevalence of 15%–30% in hypertensive young adults reported from some referral centres. Major causes of secondary hypertension include drug-induced hypertension and primary aldosteronism. Screening for secondary hypertension is thus recommended in young adults with hypertension. |
| 9.1.3   | Measurement and management of blood pressure in young adults |
|         | Out-of-office BP measurement is recommended in young adults for confirming diagnosis. The hypertension management algorithm based on CVD risk proposed in Section 6 is not fully applicable in young adults, since SCORE2 has not been validated for individuals &#x3C;40 years old. |
| 9.2     | Pregnancy |
| 9.2.1   | Definition and epidemiology |
|         | Hypertension in pregnancy is typically defined as systolic BP of ≥140 mmHg and/or diastolic BP of ≥90 mmHg. Approximately 7% of pregnancies are complicated by hypertension, of which 3% are due to pre-eclampsia and around 1% are chronic or pre-existing hypertension. |
| 9.2.2   | Classifying hypertension in pregnancy |
|         | Hypertension in pregnancy includes: Chronic hypertension, Gestational hypertension. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Managing specific patient groups**
- **Young adulthood (18–40 years)**
- Definition and epidemiology
- Discuss lifestyle guidance for BP lowering
- Discuss family planning with young women
- Emphasize adherence to treatment
- **Secondary hypertension in young adulthood**
- Screen for causes of secondary hypertension
- Investigate drug-induced hypertension
- **Measurement and management of blood pressure in young adults**
- Recommend out-of-office BP measurement
- Note that SCORE2 is not validated for &#x3C;40 years
- **Pregnancy**
- Definition and epidemiology
- Define hypertension in pregnancy
- Discuss prevalence and complications
- **Classifying hypertension in pregnancy**
- Chronic hypertension
- Gestational hypertension
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

### Markdown Table

| **Guidelines** | **Details** |
|----------------|-------------|
| Antenatally unclassifiable hypertension | BP is first recorded after 20 weeks of gestation, and hypertension is diagnosed but it is unclear if chronic or not; reassessment is necessary 6 weeks post-partum. |
| Pre-eclampsia | Gestational hypertension accompanied by: (i) proteinuria (>0.3 g/day or ≥30 mg/mmol ACR), (ii) other maternal organ dysfunction, including acute kidney injury (serum creatinine ≥ 1 mg/dL), liver dysfunction (elevated transaminases > 40 UI/L with or without right upper quadrant or epigastric abdominal pain), neurological complications (convulsions, altered mental status, blindness, stroke, severe headaches, and persistent visual scotomata), or haematological complications (platelet count &#x3C; 150,000/µL, disseminated intravascular coagulation, haemolysis), or (iii) uteroplacental dysfunction (such as foetal growth restriction, abnormal umbilical artery Doppler waveform analysis, or stillbirth). |
| Treatment initiation and blood pressure targets | Acute management of BP in pre-eclampsia and eclampsia is detailed in Section 10.4. The only cure for pre-eclampsia is delivery, which is recommended at 37 weeks’ gestation, or earlier in high-risk cases. |
| Measuring blood pressure in pregnancy | Oscillometric devices tend to under-estimate the true BP and are unreliable in severe eclampsia; only a few have been validated in pregnancy. |
| Investigating hypertension in pregnancy | Basic laboratory investigations include urinalysis, blood count, liver enzymes, serum creatinine, and serum uric acid. |
| Managing mild hypertension in pregnancy | RAS inhibitors are not recommended in pregnancy due to adverse maternal and neonatal outcomes. The BP-lowering drugs of choice are beta-blockers, dihydropyridine CCBs, and methyldopa. |
| Preventing hypertension and pre-eclampsia | Low-to-moderate-intensity exercise, especially if supervised and initiated during the first trimester of pregnancy, decreases the incidence of hypertension. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess pregnant women for hypertension.
2. **Is BP ≥ 140/90 mmHg?**
- Yes: Proceed to investigate hypertension.
- No: Monitor regularly.
3. **Investigate Hypertension**
- Conduct basic laboratory investigations (urinalysis, blood count, liver enzymes, serum creatinine, serum uric acid).
4. **Is proteinuria present?**
- Yes: Diagnose pre-eclampsia if other criteria are met.
- No: Consider other causes of hypertension.
5. **Assess for other complications**
- Check for organ dysfunction, neurological complications, or uteroplacental dysfunction.
6. **Is pre-eclampsia diagnosed?**
- Yes: Initiate treatment and plan for delivery.
- No: Manage mild hypertension.
7. **Manage Mild Hypertension**
- Prescribe BP-lowering medications (beta-blockers, CCBs, methyldopa).
8. **Monitor BP regularly**
- Adjust treatment as necessary.
9. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| Recommendations                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| In women with gestational hypertension, starting drug treatment is recommended for those with confirmed office systolic BP ≥ 140 mmHg or diastolic BP ≥ 90 mmHg.661 | I      | B      |
| In pregnant women with chronic hypertension, starting drug treatment is recommended for those with confirmed office systolic BP ≥ 140 mmHg or diastolic BP ≥ 90 mmHg.88,660,661,678 | I      | B      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Managing Severe Hypertension in Pregnancy**
- Acute onset of severe hypertension (>160/110 mmHg) persisting for more than 15 min
- Considered a hypertensive emergency
- Immediate hospitalization should be considered
- Consult with obstetrician for management

2. **Post-Partum Blood Pressure Management**
- Measure BP within 6 hours of delivery
- Daily BP monitoring for at least a week after discharge
- Consider HBPM and ABPM to exclude white-coat and masked hypertension

3. **Risk Assessment for Chronic Hypertension**
- Women with hypertension during pregnancy are at increased risk of chronic hypertension, CKD, and CVD
- Regular screening recommended in the first 6 months post-delivery
- BP measurements, urine analyses, and CVD risk assessment should be performed at 6-12 weeks, 6 months, and 12 months post-partum

4. **Management of BP-Lowering Drugs in Breastfeeding**
- All BP-lowering drugs are excreted into breast milk
- Most drugs are excreted in very low concentrations, except for propranolol, atenolol, acebutolol, and nifedipine

5. **Risk of Recurrence of Hypertensive Disorders in Subsequent Pregnancies**
- About 20%-30% of women with hypertensive disorders in a previous pregnancy will experience recurrence
- Earlier onset of hypertension in the first pregnancy increases the risk of recurrence
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Clinical Frailty Scale | Description                                                                                          |
|------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1. Very fit            | People who are robust, active, energetic and motivated. Commonly exercise regularly.                 |
| 2. Well                | People who have no active disease symptoms but are less fit than category I.                        |
| 3. Managing well       | People whose medical problems are well controlled, but are not regularly active beyond routine walking. |
| 4. Vulnerable          | While not dependent on others for daily help, often symptoms limit activities.                       |
| 5. Mildly frail       | These people often have more evident slowing, and need help in high order IADLs.                    |
| 6. Moderately frail    | People need help with all outside activities and with keeping house.                                 |
| 7. Severely frail     | Completely dependent for personal care, from whatever cause.                                         |
| 8. Very severely frail | Completely dependent, approaching the end of life.                                                  |
| 9. Terminally ill      | Approaching the end of life. This category applies to people with a life expectancy &#x3C;6 months.      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Assess Patient's Frailty Level
- Very Fit (Scale 1)
- Follow BP-lowering treatment guidelines as per younger cohorts.
- Well (Scale 2)
- Evidence for benefits in reducing CVD events with more intensive treatment.
- Managing Well (Scale 3)
- Low-dose combination therapy to achieve BP control is reasonable.
- Vulnerable (Scale 4)
- Monitor for symptomatic OH, asymptomatic OH with falls, poor treatment tolerance.
- Mildly Frail (Scale 5)
- Use clinical judgement and APBM/HBPM to guide deprescribing or medication adjustment.
- Moderately Frail (Scale 6)
- Exercise caution and clinical judgement in beginning and intensifying BP-lowering treatment.
- Severely Frail (Scale 7)
- Completely dependent for personal care.
- Very Severely Frail (Scale 8)
- Completely dependent, approaching the end of life.
- Terminally Ill (Scale 9)
- Approaching the end of life with life expectancy &#x3C;6 months.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

```markdown
| Recommendations                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| It is recommended that treatment of elevated BP and hypertension among older patients aged &#x3C;85 years who are not moderately to severely frail follows the same guidelines as for younger people, provided BP-lowering treatment is well tolerated. | I      | A      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Starting Blood Pressure-Lowering Treatment in Very Old or Frail Patients**
- Inform patients about benefits and risks of BP-lowering treatment.
- Consider patient preference.
- Start treatment if BP ≥ 140/90 mmHg and meet any of the following criteria:
- Pre-treatment symptomatic orthostatic hypotension
- Age ≥ 85 years
- Clinically significant moderate-to-severe frailty
- Limited predicted lifespan (&#x3C; 3 years)
- Preferred medications:
- Long-acting dihydropyridine calcium channel antagonist
- ACE inhibitor or ARB (if contraindicated)
- Low-dose thiazides or thiazide-like diuretics (unless contraindicated)
- Avoid beta-blockers unless compelling indications exist.
- Monitor treatment tolerance closely.

2. **Maintaining Blood Pressure-Lowering in Very Old or Frail Patients**
- If treatment is well tolerated, do not automatically deprescribe.
- Review medications regularly, especially in cases of progressive frailty.
- Consider deprescribing if systolic BP drops significantly.

3. **Deprescribing BP-Lowering Medications**
- Review current medications to identify contraindicated BP-lowering drugs.
- Use ABPM to detect orthostatic hypotension or highly variable BP.
- Consider deprescribing if necessary based on patient’s condition.

4. **Isolated Systolic Hypertension**
- Defined as systolic BP ≥ 140 mmHg and diastolic BP &#x3C; 90 mmHg.
- Most common type of hypertension in older patients.
- Systolic BP increases with age; diastolic BP increases until the fifth or sixth decade, then plateaus or decreases.
- Management aligns with guidelines for younger adults if treatment is well tolerated.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

```markdown
| Section | Description |
|---------|-------------|
| 9.4.3   | Isolated systolic hypertension in young adults poses different pathophysiological and clinical considerations. In young patients (&#x3C;40 years old), arterial stiffness and relative risk of CVD events appear similar to those without isolated systolic hypertension. |
| 9.4.4   | Isolated diastolic hypertension is defined as a systolic BP of &#x3C;140 mmHg with a diastolic BP of ≥90 mmHg. This phenotype is more commonly seen in younger adults, particularly those with obesity or metabolic derangements. |
|         | Patients with isolated diastolic hypertension have a slightly increased relative risk for CVD of 5%–30%. However, due to their younger age, they tend to have few events, requiring large samples to show this association. |
|         | The absolute risk for CVD among these individuals is low (typically &#x3C;10% over 10 years), making it unclear if isolated diastolic hypertension should prompt initiation of BP-lowering medication. |
|         | Management of orthostatic hypotension should be non-pharmacological initially, including position changes, hydration, and avoiding alcohol. Compression garments and abdominal binders may also help. |
|         | When a patient achieves a target systolic BP of 120–129 mmHg, there is little high-quality evidence that further intensifying BP-lowering medication improves CVD prognosis. |
|         | For orthostatic hypotension with supine hypertension, midodrine is the best evidence-based treatment, potentially in conjunction with ongoing BP-lowering medications. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Assess for Orthostatic Hypotension
- Measure BP in sitting/lying and standing positions.
- Timing: 1 and/or 3 minutes after standing.
- Consider supine to standing assessment if seated to standing leads to under-detection.

2. Determine if Orthostatic Hypotension is Present
- Criteria: Drop in BP of 20/10 mmHg after standing.
- Check for symptoms: dizziness, lightheadedness.

3. If Orthostatic Hypotension is Present
- Assess severity: Standing systolic BP &#x3C; 110 mmHg.
- Evaluate for underlying causes: neurogenic vs. non-neurogenic.

4. Management Strategies
- Non-pharmacological:
- Change positions slowly.
- Maintain hydration.
- Avoid alcohol and large meals.
- Consider compression garments and abdominal binders.
- If symptoms are disabling and non-pharmacological measures fail:
- Consider pharmacological options: Midodrine or Droxidopa.
- Monitor BP closely, especially supine BP.

5. Follow-Up
- Regularly monitor BP and adjust treatment as necessary.
- Consider specialist referral for complex cases.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Bảng biểu

| Recommendation                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| We recommend that all patients with diabetes are offered logical BP-lowering treatment with a BP target of 120–129/70–79 mmHg, if feasible and tolerated. | I      | B      |
| Before starting or intensifying BP-lowering medication, it is recommended to test for orthostatic hypotension, by first having the patient sit or lie for 5 min and then measuring BP 1 and/or 3 min after standing. | I      | B      |
| It is recommended to pursue non-pharmacological approaches as the first-line treatment of orthostatic hypotension among persons with supine hypertension. | I      | A      |
| For such patients, it is also recommended to switch BP-lowering medications that worsen orthostatic hypotension to an alternative BP-lowering therapy and not to simply de-intensify therapy. | I      | A      |
| BP-lowering drug treatment is recommended for people with pre-diabetes or obesity when confirmed office BP is ≥ 140/90 mmHg or when office BP is 130–139/80–89 mmHg and the patient is at predicted 10-year risk of CVD ≥10% or with high-risk conditions, despite a maximum of 3 months of lifestyle therapy. | I      | A      |
| In persons with diabetes who are receiving BP-lowering drugs, it is recommended to target systolic BP to 120–129 mmHg, if tolerated. | I      | A      |

### Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**
2. **Kiểm tra huyết áp (BP)**
- Nếu BP ≥ 130/80 mmHg:
- Tiến hành điều trị hạ BP bằng thuốc.
- Nếu BP &#x3C; 130/80 mmHg:
- Tiến hành theo dõi và can thiệp lối sống.
3. **Kiểm tra tình trạng tiểu đường**
- Nếu bệnh nhân có tiểu đường:
- Đặt mục tiêu BP 120–129/70–79 mmHg.
- Nếu không có tiểu đường:
- Đặt mục tiêu BP theo hướng dẫn chung.
4. **Kiểm tra tình trạng huyết áp tư thế**
- Nếu có hạ huyết áp tư thế:
- Thay đổi thuốc hạ BP.
- Nếu không có:
- Tiếp tục điều trị hiện tại.
5. **Kết thúc**
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| Recommendations for Managing Hypertension in Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease | Classa | Levelb |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| In patients with diabetic or non-diabetic moderate-to-severe CKD and confirmed BP ≥130/80 mmHg, lifestyle optimization and BP-lowering medication are recommended to reduce CVD risk, provided such treatment is well tolerated. | I      | A      |
| In adults with moderate-to-severe CKD who are receiving BP-lowering drugs and who have eGFR >30 mL/min/1.73 m², it is recommended to target systolic BP to 120–129 mmHg, if tolerated. | I      | A      |
| In hypertensive patients with CKD and eGFR >20 mL/min/1.73 m², SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended to improve outcomes in the context of their modest BP-lowering properties. | I      | A      |
| Patients with CKD usually require combination therapy, and this should be initiated as a combination of a RAS inhibitor and a CCB or diuretic. | IIa    | B      |
| The combination of an ACE inhibitor and an ARB is not recommended in CKD or any other BP-treatment scenario. | -      | -      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**: Assess patient with CKD and elevated BP.
- If moderate-to-severe CKD (eGFR &#x3C;60 mL/min/1.73 m² or albuminuria ≥30 mg/g):
- Proceed to BP-lowering drug therapy.
- If mild CKD:
- Conduct CVD risk assessment before treatment decision.

2. **BP-lowering in CKD**:
- If BP ≥130/80 mmHg:
- Recommend lifestyle optimization and BP-lowering medication.
- If eGFR >30 mL/min/1.73 m²:
- Target systolic BP to 120–129 mmHg if tolerated.

3. **Combination Therapy**:
- If resistant hypertension:
- Initiate combination therapy with RAS inhibitor and CCB or diuretic.
- If eGFR &#x3C;30 mL/min/1.73 m²:
- Ensure adequately up-titrated loop diuretic.

4. **SGLT2 Inhibitors**:
- If hypertensive with eGFR >20 mL/min/1.73 m²:
- Recommend SGLT2 inhibitors for improved outcomes.

5. **End**: Monitor and adjust treatment as necessary.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Section | Content |
|---------|---------|
| 9.8.2   | Coronary artery disease with particular reference to the blood pressure J-curve |
|         | Important considerations in patients with coronary artery disease are: |
|         | (i) the BP J-curve (an observation suggesting that over-intensive BP lowering may increase CVD risk in some patients), and (ii) compelling indications for specific classes of BP-lowering medications. |
|         | The J-curve phenomenon describes increased risk for CVD observed among patients with the lowest and highest BP in the dataset, with the best CVD outcome rates typically observed among those with BP in the normal range (e.g. systolic BP of 100–120 mmHg and diastolic BP of 60–80 mmHg). |
|         | For this reason, the J-curve is sometimes also called the U-curve, with both terms typically used interchangeably. |
|         | However, observational data do not consistently demonstrate a BP J-curve with CVD risk. It is more commonly observed among patients with established clinical CVD, such as those with coronary artery disease, or in secondary prevention cohorts. |
|         | Furthermore, the J-curve is more commonly observed when analysing diastolic BP values vs. systolic BP values, though it has been described for both. |
|         | This stronger relationship with diastolic BP has informed the hypothesis that the J-curve may be caused by reduced perfusion of major organs at low BP, which is particularly operative for diastolic BP in the coronary culature when considering ischaemic heart disease events. |
|         | If excessive lowering of BP causes CVD events, this needs to be addressed in treatment recommendations provided by BP management guidelines. |
|         | The 2018 ESC/ESH Guidelines on the management of arterial hypertension introduced, for the first time, lower bounds of BP-lowering treatment targets, implying that treatment be de-intensified for patients with on-treatment BP below these bounds (i.e. &#x3C;120 mmHg systolic or &#x3C;70 mmHg diastolic). |
|         | The 2023 ESH guidelines also make this argument. |
| 9.8.3   | Valvular heart disease |
|         | Most patients with both severe heart valve disease and heart failure can be considered at increased risk of CVD. Accordingly, it is recommended they are treated with BP-lowering therapy for confirmed baseline BP of >130/80 mmHg, and their recommended target of treatment is BP of 120–129/70–79 mmHg, provided treatment is tolerated. |
|         | Persons with mild-to-moderate heart valve disease should have a CVD risk assessment prior to deciding their BP-lowering treatment threshold and target. |
|         | Vasodilating ARBs/ACE inhibitors are preferable over vasodilating dihydropyridine CCBs because of the link between valvular heart disease and subsequent heart failure and given the stronger efficacy evidence for ARBs/ACE inhibitors in the setting of heart failure once manifested. |
|         | In aortic valve stenosis, concomitant hypertension influences both the aortic root, the aortic valve, and the left ventricular structure and function. In this subgroup, treatment preferably with ARBs/ACE inhibitors should be considered. A beta-blocker may be added if BP remains >140/90 mmHg. |
| 9.8.4   | Heart failure |
|         | Patients with symptomatic heart failure are at increased risk of CVD. Therefore, it is recommended that these patients are treated with BP-lowering therapy for confirmed baseline BP of >130/80 mmHg and their recommended treatment target is BP of 120–129/70–79 mmHg, provided treatment is tolerated and with out-of-office evaluation of on-treatment BP. |
|         | Of note, many patients with systolic heart failure on maximal heart failure therapies have BP of &#x3C;120/70 mmHg, and we do not recommend de-intensifying such treatment unless indicated by symptomatic side effects. |
|         | There is a limit to how low BP can be treated without potentially tipping the scales in favour of CVD harm vs. CVD benefit. However, it is not clear what that limit is and how much it differs based on comorbidities. |
|         | Currently, the data do not suggest that risk for CVD can be significantly increased by treating any patient to the recommended intensive BP target outlined in these guidelines of as low as 120/70 mmHg. |
|         | We also do not recommend stopping or de-intensifying BP-lowering medication among asymptomatic patients with on-treatment BP of &#x3C;120/70 mmHg. |
|         | It should be recognized, though, that there are no data demonstrating that an on-treatment systolic BP of &#x3C;90 mmHg or an on-treatment diastolic BP of &#x3C;50 mmHg is safe from a CVD perspective. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient Assessment**
- Evaluate for severe valvular heart disease and symptomatic heart failure.
- Determine baseline BP.

2. **BP Management Decision**
- If BP > 130/80 mmHg:
- Proceed to BP-lowering therapy.
- Target BP: 120–129/70–79 mmHg.
- If BP ≤ 130/80 mmHg:
- Assess for other indications for BP-lowering therapy.

3. **Medication Selection**
- Prefer ARBs/ACE inhibitors over dihydropyridine CCBs.
- Consider beta-blocker if BP remains > 140/90 mmHg.

4. **Monitoring and Follow-up**
- Regularly monitor BP and adjust treatment as necessary.
- Evaluate for symptomatic side effects.

5. **Considerations for Heart Failure**
- If symptomatic heart failure, continue BP-lowering therapy.
- Ensure treatment is tolerated and monitor for adverse effects.

6. **Final Assessment**
- If BP &#x3C; 120/70 mmHg and asymptomatic, do not de-intensify treatment.
- If symptomatic side effects occur, reassess treatment strategy.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Recommendations                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| In patients with a history of myocardial infarction who require BP-lowering treatment, beta-blockers and RAS blockers are recommended as part of that treatment. | I      | A      |
| In patients with symptomatic angina who require BP-lowering treatment, beta-blockers and/or CCBs are recommended as part of that treatment. | I      | A      |
| In patients with symptomatic HFrEF/HFmrEF, the following treatments with BP-lowering effects are recommended (or ARBs to improve outcomes: ACE inhibitors if ACE inhibitors are not tolerated) or ARNi, beta-blockers, MRAs, and SGLT2 inhibitors. | I      | A      |
| In hypertensive patients with symptomatic HFpEF, SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended to improve outcomes in addition to their modest BP-lowering properties. | I      | A      |
| In patients with a history of aortic valve stenosis and/or regurgitation who require BP-lowering treatment, RAS blockers should be considered as part of that treatment. | IIa    | C      |
| In patients with a history of moderate-to-severe mitral valve regurgitation who require BP-lowering treatment, RAS blockers should be considered as part of that treatment. | IIa    | C      |
| In patients with symptomatic HFpEF who have BP above target, ARBs and/or MRAs may be considered to reduce heart failure hospitalizations and reduce BP. | IIb    | B      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
2. **Patient with a history of myocardial infarction?**
- Yes: Recommend beta-blockers and RAS blockers (Class I, Level A)
- No: Proceed to next step
3. **Patient with symptomatic angina?**
- Yes: Recommend beta-blockers and/or CCBs (Class I, Level A)
- No: Proceed to next step
4. **Patient with symptomatic HFrEF/HFmrEF?**
- Yes: Recommend ACE inhibitors (if tolerated), ARNi, beta-blockers, MRAs, SGLT2 inhibitors (Class I, Level A)
- No: Proceed to next step
5. **Patient with symptomatic HFpEF?**
- Yes: Recommend SGLT2 inhibitors (Class I, Level A)
- No: Proceed to next step
6. **Patient with a history of aortic valve stenosis/regurgitation?**
- Yes: Consider RAS blockers (Class IIa, Level C)
- No: Proceed to next step
7. **Patient with a history of moderate-to-severe mitral valve regurgitation?**
- Yes: Consider RAS blockers (Class IIa, Level C)
- No: Proceed to next step
8. **Patient with symptomatic HFpEF and BP above target?**
- Yes: Consider ARBs and/or MRAs (Class IIb, Level B)
- No: **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| Recommendations                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| It is recommended that the BP-lowering drug treatment strategy for preventing recurrent stroke should comprise a RAS blocker plus a CCB or a thiazide-like diuretic. | I      | A      |
| In patients with confirmed BP ≥ 130/80 mmHg with a history of TIA or stroke, a systolic BP target of 120–129 mmHg is recommended to reduce CVD outcomes, provided treatment is tolerated. | I      | A      |
| In black patients from Sub-Saharan Africa who require BP-lowering treatment, combination therapy including a CCB combined with either a thiazide diuretic or a RAS blocker should be considered. | IIa    | B      |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
2. **Assess Patient's Condition**
- Check for history of TIA or stroke
- Check BP levels
3. **If BP ≥ 130/80 mmHg and history of TIA or stroke**
- Set target BP: 120–129 mmHg
- Proceed to treatment
4. **If not, assess for recurrent stroke prevention**
- Recommend RAS blocker + CCB or thiazide-like diuretic
5. **Assess Ethnic Background**
- If patient is black from Sub-Saharan Africa
- Recommend combination therapy: CCB + thiazide diuretic or RAS blocker
6. **Monitor Treatment Efficacy**
- Adjust treatment as necessary
7. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Bảng biểu

| Nhóm Dipping          | Mô tả                                                                                     |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Inverse dipping      | Tăng huyết áp vào ban đêm (tỷ lệ ban đêm so với ban ngày > 1.0)                        |
| Non-dipper           | Giảm huyết áp ban đêm &#x3C; 10% (tỷ lệ ban đêm so với ban ngày > 0.9 và ≤ 1.0)            |
| Normal dipping       | Giảm huyết áp ban đêm > 10% và &#x3C; 20% (tỷ lệ ban đêm so với ban ngày 0.8 đến 0.9)     |
| Extreme dipping      | Giảm huyết áp ban đêm > 20% (tỷ lệ ban đêm so với ban ngày &#x3C; 0.8)                     |

### Sơ đồ/Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**
- Xác định tình trạng huyết áp của bệnh nhân.
2. **Huyết áp ban đêm**
- Có tăng huyết áp ban đêm?
- Có: Chuyển đến bước 3.
- Không: Kết thúc.
3. **Phân loại Dipping**
- Xác định nhóm dipping:
- Inverse dipping: Tăng huyết áp vào ban đêm.
- Non-dipper: Giảm huyết áp ban đêm &#x3C; 10%.
- Normal dipping: Giảm huyết áp ban đêm > 10% và &#x3C; 20%.
- Extreme dipping: Giảm huyết áp ban đêm > 20%.
4. **Can thiệp**
- **Can thiệp không dùng thuốc**: Thay đổi lối sống (chế độ ăn uống, tập thể dục).
- **Can thiệp dùng thuốc**:
- Sử dụng thuốc hạ huyết áp.
- Xem xét thuốc lợi tiểu thiazide dài hạn.
5. **Theo dõi**
- Theo dõi huyết áp và điều chỉnh điều trị nếu cần.
6. **Kết thúc**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Bảng biểu

| Khuyến nghị                                                                 | Lớp   | Cấp   |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------|-------|
| Referral to hypertension centre should be considered                       | IIa   |       |
| Exclusion of secondary and pseudo-resistant hypertension                   |       |       |
| Treatment optimization of BP-lowering medications (ideally three-drug SPC)|       |       |
| If spironolactone is not tolerated: eplerenone                            | IIa   |       |
| Beta-blocker (if not already recommended for a compelling indication)     | IIa   |       |
| Intensification of pharmacotherapy                                         |       |       |
| Shared risk-benefit discussion                                             |       |       |
| Interventional therapy                                                     |       |       |
| Alpha blockers                                                             | IIa   |       |
| Centrally acting BP-lowering drugs                                         | IIa   |       |
| K+ sparing diuretics                                                      | IIa   |       |
| Others                                                                    | IIb   |       |
| If BP remains uncontrolled                                                 |       |       |
| In patients with resistant hypertension and uncontrolled BP despite use of first-line BP lowering therapies, the addition of spironolactone to existing treatment should be considered. | IIa   | B     |

### Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá huyết áp văn phòng
- Nếu BP ≥ 140/90 mmHg với 3 hoặc nhiều thuốc hạ huyết áp
- **Rẽ nhánh**:
- **Có**: Xem xét giới thiệu đến trung tâm huyết áp (Class IIa)
- **Không**: Tiếp tục theo dõi
2. **Loại trừ**: Huyết áp thứ phát và huyết áp giả kháng
3. **Tối ưu hóa điều trị**:
- Sử dụng thuốc hạ huyết áp (tối ưu là ba thuốc SPC)
4. **Điều trị kháng trị thực sự**:
- **Bước 1**: Thêm spironolactone
- Nếu không dung nạp: Thêm eplerenone (Class IIa)
- **Bước 2**: Thêm beta-blocker (nếu chưa được khuyến nghị cho chỉ định thuyết phục) (Class IIa)
- **Bước 3**: Tăng cường điều trị dược lý
- **Bước 4**: Thảo luận về lợi ích-rủi ro
- **Bước 5**: Đánh giá đa ngành
- **Bước 6**: Nếu BP vẫn không kiểm soát được:
- Thêm alpha blockers
- Thêm thuốc hạ huyết áp tác động trung ương
- Thêm thuốc lợi tiểu giữ K+
- Thêm các thuốc khác (Class IIb)
5. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| Recommendations                                                                                                         | Classa | Levelb |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| Renal artery angioplasty without stenting should be considered for patients with hypertension and haemodynamically significant renal artery stenosis due to fibromuscular dysplasia. | IIa    | C      |
| Renal artery angioplasty and stenting may be considered in patients with haemodynamically significant, atherosclerotic, renal artery stenosis (stenosis of 70%–99%, or 50%–69% with post-stenotic dilatation and/or significant trans-stenotic pressure gradient) with: | IIb    | C      |
| - Recurrent heart failure, unstable angina, or sudden onset flash pulmonary oedema despite maximally tolerated medical therapy; |        |        |
| - Resistant hypertension;                                                                                               |        |        |
| - Hypertension with unexplained unilaterally small kidney or CKD;                                                      |        |        |
| - Bilateral renal artery stenosis or unilateral renal artery stenosis in a solitary viable kidney.                      |        |        |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient with Resistant Hypertension**
- If spironolactone is not effective or tolerated:
- Consider treatment with eplerenone.
- Consider addition of a beta-blocker, centrally acting medication, alpha-blocker, hydralazine, or potassium-sparing diuretic.
- If BP is uncontrolled despite three BP-lowering drugs:
- Consider catheter-based renal denervation after shared risk-benefit discussion and multidisciplinary assessment.

2. **Renovascular Hypertension (RVH)**
- Start medical therapy to reduce BP.
- If fibromuscular dysplasia is present:
- Perform percutaneous transluminal renal angioplasty (PTRA) without stenting.
- If PTRA is not feasible:
- Use RAS blockers with careful monitoring of renal function.

3. **Management of Specific Causes of Secondary Hypertension**
- Identify and remove underlying causes.
- If secondary hypertension is due to primary aldosteronism:
- Determine subtype (unilateral or bilateral).
- For unilateral: Consider surgical removal of adrenal gland.
- For bilateral: Use MRAs (spironolactone or eplerenone).

4. **Considerations for Atherosclerotic Renal Artery Stenosis**
- If significant stenosis is present:
- Consider renal artery angioplasty without stenting.
- Consider renal artery angioplasty and stenting for specific conditions (e.g., recurrent heart failure, resistant hypertension).
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

### Markdown Table

| Section | Recommendation | Level of Evidence | Description |
|---------|----------------|-------------------|-------------|
| 9.14.6  | Drug-induced hypertension | IIb | Over-the-counter medications, prescribed drugs, and drug abuse can cause hypertension. |
| 9.14.6.1 | Anticancer drug-induced hypertension | III | Anticancer and anti-angiogenic drugs can cause hypertension, especially in patients treated with vascular endothelial growth factor inhibitors. |
| 9.14.4 | Phaeochromocytoma/paraganglioma | - | Phaeochromocytomas are rare adrenal tumours that secrete catecholamines and can cause hypertension. |
| 9.14.7 | Other forms of secondary hypertension | - | Other rare causes of secondary hypertension include genetic causes, Cushing’s syndrome, and acromegaly. |
| 10 | Acute and short-term lowering of blood pressure | - | Management of hypertensive emergencies requires immediate intervention to reduce BP. |
| 10.1 | Acute blood pressure management | - | Hypertensive emergency is defined as BP of ≥180/110 mmHg associated with acute HMOD. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient with Indication for Renal Artery Revascularization**
- If technically feasible: Proceed with renal artery angioplasty and stenting.
- If technically unfeasible or failed: Consider open surgical revascularization.

2. **Confirmed Haemodynamically Significant Renal Artery Stenosis**
- If confirmed: Proceed with renal artery angioplasty.
- If not confirmed: Do not recommend renal artery angioplasty.

3. **Drug-Induced Hypertension**
- Identify potential drug causes (over-the-counter, prescribed, recreational).
- Manage according to general population guidelines.

4. **Anticancer Drug-Induced Hypertension**
- Monitor patients on anticancer therapies for BP increases.
- Manage hypertension as per general population guidelines.

5. **Phaeochromocytoma/Paraganglioma**
- Identify and manage with multidisciplinary healthcare.
- Surgical excision is mandated for secreting tumours.

6. **Hypertensive Emergency**
- Define as BP ≥180/110 mmHg with acute HMOD.
- Immediate intervention required to reduce BP.

7. **Acute Blood Pressure Management**
- Assess symptoms and organ involvement.
- Administer i.v. therapy as needed.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Bảng biểu

| Khuyến nghị                                                                 | Lớp | Cấp |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
| Đối với bệnh nhân có đột quỵ thiếu máu cục bộ hoặc TIA và có chỉ định hạ huyết áp, nên bắt đầu liệu pháp hạ huyết áp trước khi xuất viện. | I   | B   |
| Trong bệnh nhân có đột quỵ thiếu máu cục bộ, nên xem xét hạ huyết áp sớm trong 24 giờ đầu trong các trường hợp sau: |     |     |
| • Trong bệnh nhân đủ điều kiện cho liệu pháp tái tưới máu bằng thrombolysis tĩnh mạch hoặc lấy huyết khối cơ học, huyết áp nên được hạ và duy trì dưới 180/105 mmHg ít nhất trong 24 giờ sau điều trị. | IIa | B   |

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân có triệu chứng tăng huyết áp.
- Nếu có tổn thương cơ quan:
- Tiến hành điều trị khẩn cấp.
- Nếu không có tổn thương cơ quan:
- Đánh giá mức độ huyết áp.
- Nếu huyết áp > 180/105 mmHg:
- Tiến hành điều trị hạ huyết áp.
- Nếu huyết áp &#x3C; 180/105 mmHg:
- Theo dõi và xem xét điều trị ngoại trú.
2. **Điều trị khẩn cấp**:
- Xác định cơ quan mục tiêu bị ảnh hưởng.
- Xác định nguyên nhân gây tăng huyết áp.
- Lên kế hoạch hạ huyết áp an toàn.
3. **Quản lý huyết áp**:
- Sử dụng thuốc hạ huyết áp tĩnh mạch cho trường hợp khẩn cấp.
- Duy trì huyết áp dưới 180/105 mmHg trong 24 giờ.
4. **Theo dõi**:
- Đánh giá lại huyết áp sau 24 giờ.
- Nếu huyết áp vẫn cao, xem xét điều trị tiếp theo.
5. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá tình trạng bệnh nhân và lên kế hoạch theo dõi lâu dài.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| Recommendation                                                                                          | Classa | Levelb |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| In patients with ischaemic stroke not receiving re-perfusion treatment and BP of ≥220/110 mmHg, BP should be carefully lowered by approximately 15% during the first 24 h after stroke onset. | IIa    | C      |
| In patients with intracerebral haemorrhage, immediate BP lowering (within 6 h of symptom onset) should be considered to a systolic target 140–160 mmHg to prevent haematoma expansion and improve functional outcome. | IIa    | A      |
| In pre-eclampsia or eclampsia with hypertensive crisis, drug treatment with i.v. labetalol or nicardipine and magnesium is recommended. | I      | C      |
| In pre-eclampsia or eclampsia associated with pulmonary oedema, nitroglycerin given as an i.v. infusion is recommended. | I      | C      |
| In severe hypertension in pregnancy: drug treatment with i.v. labetalol, oral methyldopa, or oral nifedipine is recommended. Intravenous hydralazine is a second-line option. | I      | C      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient presents with severe hypertension in pregnancy**
- If pre-eclampsia or eclampsia:
- **Consider drug treatment**: i.v. labetalol or nicardipine and magnesium
- If hypertensive crisis:
- **Administer**: i.v. labetalol or nicardipine and magnesium
- If associated with pulmonary oedema:
- **Administer**: nitroglycerin i.v. infusion
- If severe hypertension without pre-eclampsia:
- **Consider drug treatment**: i.v. labetalol, oral methyldopa, or oral nifedipine
- If BP not controlled:
- **Consider**: intravenous hydralazine as second-line option
2. **Monitor BP closely**
- If BP ≥ 220/110 mmHg:
- **Lower BP by 15% within 24 hours**
- If intracerebral haemorrhage:
- **Lower BP to 140–160 mmHg within 6 hours**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Bảng biểu

| Thông tin                                                                 | Ghi chú                                                                 |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Patient-centred care                                                     | Định nghĩa: Thái độ của nhân viên y tế phù hợp với sở thích và nhu cầu của bệnh nhân. |
| Tầm quan trọng của việc giao tiếp hậu quả điều trị                     | Cần đánh giá sự hiểu biết của bệnh nhân về rủi ro liên quan đến huyết áp. |
| Phương pháp giao tiếp rủi ro                                            | Sử dụng SCORE2 hoặc SCORE2-OP để truyền đạt rủi ro CVD trong 10 năm. |
| Khía cạnh xã hội và hành vi sức khỏe                                    | Cần xem xét trong giao tiếp giữa bệnh nhân và nhà cung cấp dịch vụ y tế. |
| Đội ngũ y tế đa ngành                                                  | Bao gồm các chuyên gia y tế khác nhau để hỗ trợ bệnh nhân.            |
| Tự chăm sóc                                                             | Khuyến khích bệnh nhân tham gia vào việc chăm sóc sức khỏe của chính họ. |

### Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân về huyết áp.
2. **Hiểu biết của bệnh nhân**:
- Có hiểu biết về rủi ro huyết áp không?
- **Có**: Tiếp tục đến bước 3.
- **Không**: Cung cấp thông tin về rủi ro và lợi ích điều trị.
3. **Truyền đạt rủi ro**:
- Sử dụng phương pháp nào?
- **SCORE2/SCORE2-OP**: Truyền đạt rủi ro CVD trong 10 năm.
- **Rủi ro tuổi**: Truyền đạt bằng 'tuổi rủi ro' hoặc 'tuổi tim'.
4. **Thảo luận về kế hoạch điều trị**:
- Bệnh nhân có đồng ý với kế hoạch không?
- **Có**: Tiến hành điều trị.
- **Không**: Thảo luận lại về kế hoạch dựa trên sở thích của bệnh nhân.
5. **Kết thúc**: Theo dõi và đánh giá sự tiến bộ của bệnh nhân.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| Recommendation                                                                 | Classa | Levelb |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------|
| An informed discussion about CVD risk and treatment benefits tailored to the needs of a patient is recommended as part of hypertension management. | I      | C      |
| Motivational interviewing should be considered for patients with hypertension at hospitals and community health centres to assist patients in controlling their BP and to enhance treatment adherence. | IIa    | B      |
| Physician–patient web communications are an effective tool that should be considered in primary care, including reporting on home BP readings. | IIa    | C      |
| Home BP measurement for managing hypertension by using self-monitored BP is recommended to achieve better BP control. | I      | B      |
| Self-measurement, when properly performed, is recommended due to positive effects on the acceptance of a diagnosis of hypertension, patient empowerment, and adherence to treatment. | I      | C      |
| Enhanced self-monitoring of BP using a device paired with a connected smartphone application may be considered, though evidence to date suggests that this may be no more effective than standard self-monitoring. | IIb    | B      |
| Multidisciplinary approaches in the management of patients with elevated BP and hypertension, including appropriate and safe task-shifting away from physicians, are recommended to improve BP control. | I      | A      |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Begin the process of managing hypertension.

2. **Assess Patient's CVD Risk**
- Conduct an informed discussion about CVD risk and treatment benefits tailored to the patient's needs.
- **Decision Point**: Is the patient adherent to treatment?
- Yes: Proceed to monitoring.
- No: Consider motivational interviewing.

3. **Motivational Interviewing**
- Implement motivational interviewing techniques for patients struggling with adherence.
- **Outcome**: Enhance treatment adherence.

4. **Monitor BP**
- Use physician–patient web communications to report home BP readings.
- **Decision Point**: Is BP controlled?
- Yes: Continue current management.
- No: Reassess treatment plan.

5. **Self-Measurement of BP**
- Recommend home BP measurement using self-monitored BP.
- **Decision Point**: Is self-measurement performed correctly?
- Yes: Proceed to patient empowerment.
- No: Provide training on proper self-measurement techniques.

6. **Patient Empowerment**
- Encourage self-management and adherence to treatment.
- **Outcome**: Improved patient outcomes.

7. **Enhanced Self-Monitoring**
- Consider using devices paired with smartphone applications for enhanced self-monitoring.
- **Decision Point**: Is this more effective than standard self-monitoring?
- Yes: Implement enhanced self-monitoring.
- No: Continue with standard self-monitoring.

8. **Multidisciplinary Management**
- Implement a collaborative approach involving a multidisciplinary team.
- **Outcome**: Improved BP control through task-shifting and team-based care.

9. **End**
- Conclude the hypertension management process.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Bảng biểu

| Yếu tố                                 | Mô tả                                                                                     |
|----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Yếu tố kinh tế xã hội**             | Ví dụ: khả năng chi trả cho thuốc và thiếu hỗ trợ xã hội để quản lý thuốc                 |
| **Yếu tố hệ thống y tế/chăm sóc sức khỏe** | Ví dụ: sự rõ ràng và nhất quán trong giao tiếp với bệnh nhân, chất lượng mối quan hệ ongoing với nhà cung cấp dịch vụ y tế |
| **Yếu tố liên quan đến tình trạng**   | Ví dụ: tăng huyết áp là một bệnh không có triệu chứng (asymptomatic) và thường là một trong nhiều bệnh lý đi kèm |
| **Yếu tố liên quan đến bệnh nhân**    | Ví dụ: niềm tin về tăng huyết áp và thuốc liên quan, thói quen uống thuốc và căng thẳng cảm xúc |

### Lưu đồ

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**
- Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp của bệnh nhân.

2. **Huyết áp bình thường?**
- Có: Theo dõi định kỳ.
- Không: Tiến hành bước tiếp theo.

3. **Huyết áp cao?**
- Có: Đánh giá các yếu tố nguy cơ.
- Không: Theo dõi định kỳ.

4. **Yếu tố nguy cơ cao?**
- Có: Bắt đầu điều trị.
- Không: Tư vấn lối sống lành mạnh.

5. **Bắt đầu điều trị**
- Chọn thuốc hạ huyết áp phù hợp.
- Theo dõi phản ứng và tác dụng phụ.

6. **Phản ứng tốt?**
- Có: Tiếp tục điều trị.
- Không: Xem xét thay đổi thuốc hoặc điều trị bổ sung.

7. **Theo dõi định kỳ**
- Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị và điều chỉnh nếu cần.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Recommendation                                                                                                           | Level |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------|
| Out-of-office BP measurement is recommended for diagnostic purposes, particularly because it can detect both white-coat hypertension and masked hypertension. | I     B |
| Where out-of-office measurements are not logistically and/or economically feasible, then it is recommended that the diagnosis be confirmed with a repeat office BP measurement using the correct standardized measurement technique. | I     B |
| It is recommended that office BP should be measured in both arms at least at the first visit, because a between-arm systolic BP difference of >10 mmHg is associated with an increased CVD risk and may indicate arterial stenosis. | I     B |
| If a between-arm difference of >10 mmHg in systolic BP is recorded, then it is recommended that all subsequent BP readings use the arm with the higher BP reading. | I     B |
| Out-of-office BP measurement is recommended for ongoing management to quantify the effects of treatment and guide BP-lowering medication titration, and/or identify possible causes of side effects (e.g. symptomatic hypotension). | I     B |
| Where out-of-office measurements are not logistically and/or economically feasible then ongoing management is recommended to be based on repeated office BP measurements using the correct standardized measurement technique. | I     B |
| It is recommended that all patients undergoing BP measurement also undergo pulse palpation at rest to determine heart rate and arrhythmias such as AF. | I     C |
| It is recommended that BP be categorized as non-elevated BP, elevated BP, and hypertension to aid treatment decisions. | I     B |
| It is recommended to use a risk-based approach in the treatment of elevated BP, and individuals with moderate or severe CKD, established CVD, HMOD, diabetes mellitus, or familial hypercholesterolaemia are considered at increased risk for CVD events. | I     B |
| SCORE2 is recommended for assessing 10-year risk of fatal and non-fatal CVD among individuals aged 40–69 years with elevated BP who are not already considered at increased risk due to moderate or severe CKD, established CVD, HMOD, diabetes mellitus, or familial hypercholesterolaemia. | I     B |
| SCORE2-OP is recommended for assessing the 10-year risk of fatal and non-fatal CVD among individuals aged ≥70 years with elevated BP who are not already considered at increased risk due to moderate or severe CKD, established CVD, HMOD, diabetes mellitus, or familial hypercholesterolaemia. | I     B |
| It is recommended that, irrespective of age, individuals with elevated BP and a SCORE2 or SCORE2-OP CVD risk of ≥10% be considered at increased risk for CVD for the purposes of risk-based management of their elevated BP. | I     B |
| In individuals with increased CVD risk where their screening office BP is 120–139/70–89 mmHg, it is recommended to measure BP out-of-office, using ABPM and/or HBPM or, if not logistically feasible, make repeated office BP measurements on more than one visit. | I     B |
| Where screening office BP is 140–159/90–99 mmHg, it is recommended that the diagnosis of hypertension should be based on out-of-office BP measurement with ABPM and/or HBPM. If these measurements are not logistically or economically feasible, then diagnosis can be made on repeated office BP measurements on more than one visit. | I     B |
| Where screening BP is ≥160/100 mmHg: It is recommended that BP 160–179/100–109 mmHg is confirmed as soon as possible (e.g. within 1 month) preferably by either home or ambulatory BP measurements. | I     C |
| It is recommended when BP is ≥180/110 mmHg that hypertensive emergency be excluded. | I     C |
| It is recommended to measure serum creatinine, eGFR, and urine ACR in all patients with hypertension. | I     A |
| If moderate-to-severe CKD is diagnosed, it is recommended to repeat measurements of serum creatinine, eGFR, and urine ACR at least annually. | I     C |
| A 12-lead ECG is recommended for all patients with hypertension. | I     B |
| Echocardiography is recommended in patients with hypertension and ECG abnormalities, or signs or symptoms of cardiac disease. | I     B |
| Fundoscopy is recommended if BP >180/110 mmHg in the work-up of hypertensive emergency and malignant hypertension, as well as in hypertensive patients with diabetes. | I     C |
| Routine genetic testing for patients with hypertension is not recommended. | III   C |
| It is recommended that patients with hypertension presenting with suggestive signs, symptoms, or medical history of secondary hypertension are appropriately screened for secondary hypertension. | I     B |
| Restriction of sodium to approximately 2 g per day is recommended where possible in all adults with elevated BP and hypertension. | I     A |
| Moderate-intensity aerobic exercise of ≥150 min/week is recommended and should be complemented with low- or moderate-intensity dynamic or isometric resistance training (2–3 times/week) to reduce BP and CVD risk. | I     A |
| It is recommended to aim for a stable and healthy BMI (20–25 kg/m²) and waist circumference values (94 cm in men and 80 cm in women) to reduce BP and CVD risk. | I     A |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Out-of-office BP measurement recommended for diagnosis**
- Detects white-coat and masked hypertension.
- If not feasible, confirm with repeat office BP measurement.

2. **Measure office BP in both arms at first visit**
- If difference >10 mmHg, use higher reading for subsequent measurements.

3. **Ongoing management**
- Out-of-office BP measurement recommended for treatment effects and side effects.
- If not feasible, use repeated office BP measurements.

4. **Screening BP categories**
- Non-elevated BP
- Elevated BP
- Hypertension

5. **Risk-based treatment approach**
- Individuals with CKD, CVD, HMOD, diabetes, or familial hypercholesterolaemia at increased risk.

6. **Risk assessment tools**
- SCORE2 for ages 40-69.
- SCORE2-OP for ages ≥70.
- Consider increased risk if SCORE2 or SCORE2-OP ≥10%.

7. **Diagnosing hypertension**
- BP 120-139/70-89 mmHg: Out-of-office measurement recommended.
- BP 140-159/90-99 mmHg: Diagnosis based on out-of-office measurement.
- BP ≥160/100 mmHg: Confirm BP 160-179/100-109 mmHg within 1 month; exclude hypertensive emergency for BP ≥180/110 mmHg.

8. **Investigations**
- Measure serum creatinine, eGFR, urine ACR.
- ECG for all patients.
- Echocardiography for those with abnormalities or symptoms.
- Fundoscopy if BP >180/110 mmHg.

9. **Screen for secondary hypertension**
- If suggestive signs or symptoms present.

10. **Lifestyle recommendations**
- Sodium restriction to ~2g/day.
- Moderate-intensity aerobic exercise ≥150 min/week.
- Aim for healthy BMI and waist circumference.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

```markdown
| Recommendation                                                                                                           | Level |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------|
| Adopting a healthy and balanced diet such as the Mediterranean or DASH diets is recommended to help reduce BP and CVD risk. | I     |
| Men and women are recommended to drink less alcohol than the upper limit, which is about 100 g/week of pure alcohol. How this translates into number of drinks depends on portion size (the standards of which differ per country), but most drinks contain 8–14 g of alcohol per drink. Preferably, it is recommended to avoid alcohol consumption to achieve best health outcomes. | I     |
| It is recommended to restrict free sugar consumption, in particular sugar-sweetened beverages, to a maximum of 10% of energy intake. It is also recommended to discourage consumption of sugar-sweetened beverages, such as soft drinks and fruit juices, starting at young age. | I     |
| It is recommended to stop tobacco smoking, initiate supportive care, and refer to smoking cessation programmes, as tobacco use strongly and independently causes CVD, CVD events, and all-cause mortality. | I     |
| Among all BP-lowering drugs, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, dihydropyridine CCBs, and diuretics (thiazides and thiazide-like drugs such as chlorthalidone and indapamide) have demonstrated the most effective reduction of BP and CVD events, and are therefore recommended as first-line treatments to lower BP. | I     |
| It is recommended that beta-blockers are combined with any of the other major BP-lowering drug classes when there are other compelling indications for their use, e.g. angina, post-myocardial infarction, HFrEF, or for heart rate control. | I     |
| It is recommended to take medications at the most convenient time of day for the patient to establish a habitual pattern of medication taking to improve adherence. | I     |
| Given trial evidence for more effective BP control vs. monotherapy, combination BP-lowering treatment is recommended for most patients with confirmed hypertension (BP ≥ 140/90 mmHg) as initial therapy. Preferred combinations are a RAS blocker (either an ACE inhibitor or an ARB) with a dihydropyridine CCB or diuretic. Exceptions to consider include patients aged ≥ 85 years, symptomatic orthostatic hypotension, moderate-to-severe frailty, and those with elevated BP (systolic BP 120–139 mmHg or diastolic BP 70–89 mmHg) with a concomitant indication for treatment. | I     |
| In patients receiving combination BP-lowering treatment, fixed-dose single-pill combination treatment is recommended. | I     |
| If BP is not controlled with a two-drug combination, increasing to a three-drug combination is recommended, usually a RAS blocker with a dihydropyridine CCB and a thiazide/thiazide-like diuretic, and preferably in a single-pill combination. | I     |
| Combining two RAS blockers (ACE inhibitor and an ARB) is not recommended. | III   |
| In adults with elevated BP and low/medium CVD risk (&#x3C;10% over 10 years), BP lowering with lifestyle measures is recommended and can reduce the risk of CVD. | I     |
| In adults with elevated BP and sufficiently high CVD risk, after 3 months of lifestyle intervention, BP lowering with pharmacological treatment is recommended for those with confirmed BP ≥130/80 mmHg to reduce CVD risk. | I     |
| It is recommended that in hypertensive patients with confirmed BP ≥140/90 mmHg, irrespective of CVD risk, lifestyle measures and pharmacological BP-lowering treatment is initiated promptly to reduce CVD risk. | I     |
| It is recommended to maintain BP-lowering drug treatment lifelong, even beyond the age of 85 years, if well tolerated. | I     |
| To reduce CVD risk, it is recommended that treated systolic BP values in most adults be targeted to 120–129 mmHg, provided the treatment is well tolerated. | I     |
| In cases where BP-lowering treatment is poorly tolerated and achieving a target systolic of 120–129 mmHg is not possible, it is recommended to target a systolic BP level that is ‘as low as reasonably achievable’ (ALARA principle). | I     |
| Due to a lack of adequately powered outcomes trials demonstrating its safety and CVD benefits, renal denervation is not recommended as a first-line BP-lowering intervention for hypertension. | III   |
| Renal denervation is not recommended for treating hypertension in patients with moderate-to-severely impaired renal function (eGFR &#x3C;40 mL/min/1.73 m²) or secondary causes of hypertension, until further evidence becomes available. | III   |
| Comprehensive screening for the main causes of secondary hypertension is recommended in adults diagnosed with hypertension before the age of 40 years, except for obese young adults where it is recommended to start with an obstructive sleep apnoea evaluation. | I     |
| In women with gestational hypertension, starting drug treatment is recommended for those with confirmed office diastolic BP ≥ 90 mmHg or systolic BP ≥ 140 mmHg. | I     |
| In pregnant women with chronic hypertension, starting drug treatment is recommended for those with confirmed office systolic BP ≥140 mmHg or diastolic BP ≥90 mmHg. | I     |
| In women with chronic and gestational hypertension, it is recommended to lower BP below 140/90 mmHg but not below 80 mmHg for diastolic BP. | I     |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess patient for hypertension.
- Determine if lifestyle changes are needed.

2. **Lifestyle Changes**
- Recommend Mediterranean or DASH diet.
- Advise on alcohol consumption (limit to 100 g/week).
- Encourage reduction of free sugar intake (max 10% of energy).
- Promote cessation of tobacco use.

3. **Medication Assessment**
- If BP ≥ 140/90 mmHg, consider pharmacological treatment.
- Choose first-line medications: ACE inhibitors, ARBs, dihydropyridine CCBs, diuretics.

4. **Combination Therapy**
- If monotherapy is ineffective, consider combination therapy.
- Preferred combinations: RAS blocker with dihydropyridine CCB or diuretic.
- If needed, escalate to three-drug combination.

5. **Special Considerations**
- For patients aged ≥ 85 years or with specific conditions, adjust treatment approach.
- Monitor for tolerability and adjust BP targets accordingly.

6. **Long-term Management**
- Maintain lifelong treatment if well tolerated.
- Target systolic BP of 120–129 mmHg if possible.

7. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

### Bảng biểu

| Tình trạng bệnh | Khuyến nghị | Cấp độ | Chứng cứ |
|------------------|-------------|--------|----------|
| Hypertension in pregnancy | Dihydropyridine CCBs (preferably extended-release nifedipine), labetalol, and methyldopa are recommended first-line BP-lowering medications | I | C |
| Hypertension in pregnancy | Low- to moderate-intensity exercise is recommended to reduce the risk of gestational hypertension and pre-eclampsia | I | B |
| Hypertension in pregnancy | RAS blockers are not recommended during pregnancy | III | B |
| Elevated BP in older patients | Treatment of elevated BP and hypertension among older patients aged 85 years who are not moderately to severely frail follows the same guidelines as for younger people | I | A |
| Elevated BP in older patients | Maintain BP-lowering drug treatment lifelong, even beyond the age of 85 years, if well tolerated | I | A |
| Orthostatic hypotension | Test for orthostatic hypotension before starting or intensifying BP-lowering medication | I | B |
| Orthostatic hypotension | Pursue non-pharmacological approaches as the first-line treatment | I | A |
| Diabetes | BP lowering with pharmacological treatment is recommended for those with confirmed BP ≥130/80 mmHg | I | A |
| Diabetes | BP-lowering drug treatment is recommended for people with pre-diabetes or obesity when confirmed office BP is ≥140/90 mmHg | I | A |
| Diabetes | Target systolic BP to 120–129 mmHg in persons with diabetes receiving BP-lowering drugs, if tolerated | I | A |
| Chronic kidney disease | Lifestyle optimization and BP-lowering medication are recommended for patients with moderate-to-severe CKD and confirmed BP ≥130/80 mmHg | I | A |
| Chronic kidney disease | Target systolic BP to 120–129 mmHg in adults with moderate-to-severe CKD receiving BP-lowering drugs and eGFR 30 mL/min/1.73 m² | I | A |
| Chronic kidney disease | SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended for hypertensive patients with CKD and eGFR >20 mL/min/1.73 m² | I | A |
| Cardiac disease | Beta-blockers and RAS blockers are recommended for patients with a history of myocardial infarction requiring BP-lowering treatment | I | A |
| Cardiac disease | Beta-blockers and/or CCBs are recommended for patients with symptomatic angina requiring BP-lowering treatment | I | A |
| Cardiac disease | ACE inhibitors (or ARBs if ACE inhibitors are not tolerated), beta-blockers, MRAs, and SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended for symptomatic HFrEF/HFmrEF | I | A |
| Cardiac disease | SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended for hypertensive patients with symptomatic HFpEF | I | A |
| Other conditions | BP-lowering drug treatment strategy for preventing stroke should comprise a RAS blocker plus a CCB or a thiazide-like diuretic | I | A |
| Other conditions | Systolic BP target 120–129 mmHg is recommended for patients with confirmed BP ≥ 130/80 mmHg with a history of TIA or stroke | I | A |
| Other conditions | Renal artery angioplasty is not recommended in patients without confirmed haemodynamically significant renal artery stenosis | III | A |
| Acute and short-term lowering of blood pressure | BP lowering therapy should be commenced before hospital discharge for patients with ischaemic stroke or TIA | I | B |
| Acute and short-term lowering of blood pressure | Acute reduction in systolic BP >70 mmHg from initial levels within 1 h of commencing treatment is not recommended for patients with intracerebral haemorrhage presenting with systolic BP ≥ 220 mmHg | III | B |

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp.
- Nếu huyết áp ≥ 130/80 mmHg:
- **Đối với bệnh nhân tiểu đường**:
- Khuyến nghị điều trị bằng thuốc hạ huyết áp.
- Mục tiêu huyết áp: 120–129 mmHg nếu dung nạp.
- **Đối với bệnh nhân có tiền sử đột quỵ hoặc TIA**:
- Mục tiêu huyết áp: 120–129 mmHg.
- **Đối với bệnh nhân CKD**:
- Khuyến nghị điều trị bằng thuốc hạ huyết áp.
- Mục tiêu huyết áp: 120–129 mmHg nếu eGFR ≥ 30 mL/min/1.73 m².
- Nếu huyết áp &#x3C; 130/80 mmHg:
- **Theo dõi và đánh giá định kỳ**.
2. **Đối với bệnh nhân cao tuổi (≥ 85 tuổi)**:
- Nếu không yếu:
- Theo dõi và điều trị như người trẻ tuổi.
- Nếu yếu:
- Cân nhắc điều trị không dùng thuốc.
3. **Đối với bệnh nhân có triệu chứng HFrEF/HFmrEF**:
- Khuyến nghị điều trị bằng ACE inhibitors hoặc ARBs, beta-blockers, MRAs, SGLT2 inhibitors.
4. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị và điều chỉnh nếu cần.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

### Markdown Table

| Condition/Recommendation                                                                 | Level | Evidence |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------|----------|
| In pre-eclampsia or eclampsia with hypertensive crisis, drug treatment with i.v. labetalol or nicardipine and magnesium is recommended. | I     | C        |
| In pre-eclampsia or eclampsia associated with pulmonary oedema, nitroglycerin given as an i.v. infusion is recommended. | I     | C        |
| In severe hypertension in pregnancy: drug treatment with i.v. labetalol, oral methyldopa, or oral nifedipine is recommended. Intravenous hydralazine is a second-line option. | I     | C        |
| An informed discussion about CVD risk and treatment benefits tailored to the needs of a patient is recommended as part of hypertension management. | I     | C        |
| Home BP measurement for managing hypertension by using self-monitored BP is recommended to achieve better BP control. | I     | B        |
| Self-measurement, when properly performed, is recommended due to positive effects on the acceptance of a diagnosis of hypertension, patient empowerment, and adherence to treatment. | I     | C        |
| Multidisciplinary approaches in the management of patients with elevated BP and hypertension, including appropriate and safe task-shifting away from physicians are recommended to improve BP control. | I     | A        |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Severe Hypertension in Pregnancy**
- If pre-eclampsia or eclampsia with hypertensive crisis:
- **Treatment**: i.v. labetalol or nicardipine and magnesium
- If pre-eclampsia or eclampsia with pulmonary oedema:
- **Treatment**: Nitroglycerin as i.v. infusion
- If severe hypertension in pregnancy:
- **Treatment**: i.v. labetalol, oral methyldopa, or oral nifedipine
- **Second-line option**: Intravenous hydralazine

2. **Patient-Centred Care in Hypertension**
- **Discussion**: Informed discussion about CVD risk and treatment benefits
- **Home BP Measurement**: Recommended for better BP control
- **Self-Measurement**: Recommended for acceptance of diagnosis, empowerment, and adherence
- **Multidisciplinary Approaches**: Recommended for improved BP control
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân có triệu chứng huyết áp cao.
- Nếu có triệu chứng: Tiến hành đo huyết áp.
- Nếu không có triệu chứng: Tiến hành kiểm tra định kỳ.

2. **Đo huyết áp**:
- Nếu huyết áp ≥ 140/90 mmHg: Chẩn đoán tăng huyết áp.
- Nếu huyết áp &#x3C; 140/90 mmHg: Theo dõi và kiểm tra lại sau.

3. **Chẩn đoán tăng huyết áp**:
- Phân loại theo mức độ:
- Tăng huyết áp nhẹ (140-159/90-99 mmHg)
- Tăng huyết áp vừa (160-179/100-109 mmHg)
- Tăng huyết áp nặng (≥ 180/≥ 110 mmHg)

4. **Điều trị**:
- Tăng huyết áp nhẹ: Khuyến nghị thay đổi lối sống.
- Tăng huyết áp vừa: Bắt đầu điều trị thuốc.
- Tăng huyết áp nặng: Điều trị khẩn cấp và theo dõi chặt chẽ.

5. **Theo dõi**:
- Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị sau 1 tháng.
- Điều chỉnh thuốc nếu cần thiết.

6. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá lại định kỳ và điều chỉnh kế hoạch điều trị.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Bắt đầu
- Xác định bệnh nhân có nguy cơ cao mắc tăng huyết áp.
- Thực hiện đo huyết áp tại văn phòng.
- Nếu huyết áp ≥ 140/90 mmHg, tiếp tục.
2. Đo huyết áp tại nhà
- Khuyến khích bệnh nhân tự đo huyết áp tại nhà.
- Hướng dẫn cách sử dụng thiết bị đo huyết áp.
3. Phân tích kết quả
- Nếu kết quả đo tại nhà cho thấy huyết áp bình thường, theo dõi.
- Nếu huyết áp vẫn cao, xem xét điều trị.
4. Lập kế hoạch điều trị
- Bắt đầu điều trị bằng thuốc nếu cần thiết.
- Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị sau 1-3 tháng.
5. Theo dõi và điều chỉnh
- Theo dõi huyết áp định kỳ.
- Điều chỉnh liều thuốc hoặc thay đổi phương pháp điều trị nếu cần.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
2. **Assess Blood Pressure**
- If normal: Continue monitoring.
- If elevated: Proceed to next step.
3. **Determine Treatment Options**
- Lifestyle changes
- Pharmacological interventions
4. **Monitor Patient Response**
- If response is adequate: Continue current treatment.
- If response is inadequate: Adjust treatment plan.
5. **Follow-Up**
- Schedule regular follow-ups to reassess blood pressure and treatment efficacy.
6. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
2. **Assess Patient's Risk Factors**
- If high risk, proceed to step 3.
- If low risk, monitor and reassess periodically.
3. **Determine Treatment Strategy**
- If pharmacological treatment is needed, proceed to step 4.
- If lifestyle changes are sufficient, implement lifestyle modifications.
4. **Initiate Pharmacological Treatment**
- Choose appropriate antihypertensive medication based on guidelines.
5. **Monitor Patient's Response**
- If effective, continue treatment.
- If ineffective, reassess and adjust treatment plan.
6. **Follow-Up and Reassess Risk**
- Schedule regular follow-ups to monitor cardiovascular risk and treatment efficacy.
7. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu Đồ Chẩn Đoán/Điều Trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bước 1**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân
- Tiền sử bệnh
- Triệu chứng hiện tại
- Yếu tố nguy cơ

2. **Bước 2**: Thực hiện các xét nghiệm cần thiết
- Xét nghiệm máu
- Siêu âm tim
- Điện tâm đồ

3. **Bước 3**: Phân tích kết quả
- Nếu kết quả bình thường:
- Tiếp tục theo dõi
- Nếu có dấu hiệu bệnh lý:
- Chuyển sang bước 4

4. **Bước 4**: Lập kế hoạch điều trị
- Điều trị nội khoa
- Điều trị ngoại khoa
- Thay đổi lối sống

5. **Bước 5**: Theo dõi và đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị
- Đánh giá định kỳ
- Điều chỉnh kế hoạch điều trị nếu cần
[END_FLOWCHART]
